Problem sheet 5 · Regulate a state and estimate it from data

Calculate an optimal feedback policy and decide when filtering or sensor placement limits its implementation.

1. Energy and effort on an integrator

For \(\dot x=u\), minimise \(\int_0^\infty(9x^2+4u^2)\,dt\). (a) Find the stabilising Riccati solution and feedback gain. (b) For \(x_0=2\) calculate the optimal cost directly from the exponential trajectory. (c) If \(|u|\le2\), identify a region where the unconstrained policy is feasible.

Check the cost with a direct integral

The algebraic equation \(9-P^2/4=0\) gives \(P=6\); \(u=-1.5x\). Then \(x=2e^{-1.5t}\) and the integrand is \(72e^{-3t}\). Its integral 24 agrees with \(Px_0^2\). Feasibility is guaranteed for \(|x_0|\le4/3\) since the magnitude then decreases monotonically. The initial state 2 is outside this region.

2. A scalar filter through a missed measurement

A random-walk model has initial predicted mean 8, variance 4, and measurement variance 1. A reading of 10 arrives. (a) Calculate the corrected mean and variance. (b) Predict one step with process variance 0.5. (c) One reading is then lost. Find the gain at the following available reading.

Follow the prediction and correction chronology

The first gain is \(4/5\), so the corrected mean is 9.6 and variance 0.8. The next prior mean is 9.6 and variance 1.3. With no reading there is no correction; another prediction raises variance to 1.8. The next available reading obtains gain \(1.8/2.8=0.643\). Setting a missed reading equal to its prediction and carrying out a normal update would falsely shrink the variance.

3. An apparently quiet but unreliable sensor

A scalar innovation sequence is \(2.0,2.0,1.8,2.2,2.0\) in sensor units, with predicted innovation standard deviation 0.5. (a) Compute the standardised mean and range. (b) Compare two mechanisms that could generate the trace. (c) Propose a test that separates the mechanisms.

Use sign and time structure as well as magnitude

The standardised values are \(4,4,3.6,4.4,4\), with mean 4 and range 3.6 to 4.4. A biased sensor and a missing nearly constant load can both produce a sustained signed discrepancy. During a known calibration hold compare the device with an independent sensor. Then vary the load while holding sensor conditions fixed; innovations that follow the load implicate the model. Simply increasing the assumed noise variance could hide both effects.

Chapter 11 · Interacting actuators, pairing and decoupling

Predict how closing one loop changes the apparent gain of another, and identify a physically expensive actuator combination.

From first principles

Eliminate the second actuator to find a constrained gain

With several actuators and outputs, a gain depends on what the other controller holds fixed. Consider small steady deviations described by a square two-by-two gain matrix \(G\). First hold \(u_2\) fixed at zero. The direct response of \(y_1\) to \(u_1\) has slope \(g_{11}\). Now assume an ideal second controller adjusts \(u_2\) slowly enough to maintain \(y_2=0\) in steady state.

\[y_1=g_{11}u_1+g_{12}u_2,\]
\[0=y_2=g_{21}u_1+g_{22}u_2 .\]

For \(g_{22}\neq0\), the second actuator must move by \(-g_{21}u_1/g_{22}\). Substitution gives the constrained gain seen by the first loop and the ratio of the direct to constrained gains.

\[\left.\frac{y_1}{u_1}\right|_{y_2=0}=\frac{\det G}{g_{22}},\]
\[\lambda_{11}=\frac{g_{11}g_{22}}{\det G}.\]

The ratio requires a nonsingular matrix. A value near unity means closing the second loop changes this steady gain little. A negative value signals a reversal of the gain sign under the constraint. Dynamic phase and actuator limits still affect whether the pairing is usable.

Derivation and applications

Determine how actuator limits reshape the reachable demands

A two-actuator mechanism may have abundant force in either output separately but be unable to deliver the two requested forces simultaneously. Work with a static local model and identical actuator units. Choose outputs as the sum \(y_1\) and difference \(y_2\) of two actuator forces \(u_1,u_2\). Assume each is bidirectional and bounded by magnitude \(U\), and the static gain holds over the region of interest. The forward and inverse maps are

\[y_1=u_1+u_2,\]
\[y_2=u_1-u_2,\]
\[u_1=\frac{y_1+y_2}{2},\]
\[u_2=\frac{y_1-y_2}{2}.\]

Substitution of the inverse map into the two component limits gives a pair of strips in output space. Their intersection is the reachable region. The maximum of the absolute sum and absolute difference of two real numbers is their absolute-value sum, which simplifies the description to a diamond.

\[|y_1+y_2|\leq 2U,\]
\[|y_1-y_2|\leq 2U\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad |y_1|+|y_2|\leq2U .\]

A supervisor can retain the relative amounts of the two requested outputs by scaling a nonzero desired vector \(d\) by one factor. The largest such factor up to unity follows by placing the scaled request on the reachable boundary whenever the original request exceeds capacity.

\[y=\alpha d,\]
\[\alpha=\min\!\left(1,\frac{2U}{|d_1|+|d_2|}\right).\]

This allocation preserves the requested direction. If one output has priority, it may be preferable to maintain that output and use only the residual capacity for the other. The physical feasible set is the same for both decisions.

Worked example

Preserve the direction of a combined request

Two linear actuators each permit 50.0 N. A requested combination \((80,60)\) N would need \((70,10)\) N. Scaling by \(100/140=0.7143\) yields outputs \((57.1,42.9)\) N and actuator loads \((50.0,7.14)\) N. Checking the inverse transformation shows exactly which actuator has reached its limit while the other retains substantial headroom.

Second application

Preserve a preload while alternating the differential force

A separate rig with \(U=30.0\) N requires total preload \(y_1=44.0\) N throughout a test. The reachable inequality leaves \(|y_2|\leq60.0-44.0=16.0\) N. At the positive extreme the actuator forces are \((30.0,14.0)\) N; at the negative extreme they exchange roles. A demanded 20.0 N alternating differential amplitude would require reducing the fixed preload to at most 40.0 N.

A second loop changes the experiment

Imagine two thermal zones with separate heaters and two temperature sensors. At steady state let \(y=G u\), with \(G=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) K per unit power. Increasing heater 1 with heater 2 fixed raises both temperatures. Increasing heater 1 while actively holding temperature 2 fixed is a different experiment: heater 2 must back off. The effective gain in the first channel changes. This difference matters when a controller is tuned with its neighbour disconnected and then commissioned with both loops active.

For a general nonsingular two-by-two matrix hold \(y_2=0\). Solving \(g_{21}u_1+g_{22}u_2=0\) gives \(y_1/u_1=g_{11}-g_{12}g_{21}/g_{22}\). The ratio of the fixed-input gain to this fixed-output gain is \(g_{11}g_{22}/\det G\). More generally the relative gain array has entries \(\Lambda_{ij}=g_{ij}(G^{-1})_{ji}\). Its rows and columns each sum to one. Large magnitudes suggest cancellation. A negative relative gain indicates that the paired steady response changes sign when the other output is perfectly held, an important warning for independent-loop tuning.

Compare candidate pairings

For the two-zone matrix above the diagonal relative gains are \(4/3\) and the off-diagonal gains are \(-1/3\). The direct pairing has fixed-input gain 2 and fixed-other-output gain 1.5. The cross-pairing changes sign. These steady results do not include transport delays or transient coupling. If the direct heater path is very slow but the cross path changes quickly, a frequency-dependent model is needed around the intended control bandwidth.

Diagonalise a symmetric plant by physical combinations

Commanding both heaters together excites the common direction \((1,1)\) with gain 3; commanding them oppositely excites \((1,-1)\) with gain 1. For the desired temperature increment \((1,-1)\), exactly equal and opposite input increments \((1,-1)\) are required. Negative input here is a reduction from a positive bias; a heater already at zero cannot supply it. Inspecting a transformed system without retaining the original actuator limits can make an impossible target seem easy.

Now bring the gains closer together: \(G=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0.98\\0.98&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). The common gain is 1.98, while the differential gain is just 0.02. A unit differential target requires differential input of magnitude 50 in each actuator. Static inversion \(u=G^{-1}v\) algebraically decouples the two commands but amplifies measurement, calibration and modelling errors along that weak direction. If both temperature sensors move together during every available test, the weak direction is also particularly hard to identify.

Choose a practical validation test

First perturb each actuator separately about the operating bias and measure both outputs. Repeat at different loads and frequencies. Next excite the predicted weakest input combination within safe operating limits and check whether the differential output emerges above the noise. A design can prioritise the common temperature and relax allowable imbalance when the plant is inherently hard to split. A constrained controller can express this trade directly by penalising both output errors while respecting individual actuator bounds.

Find difficult demand directions with a singular-value decomposition

Relative gains help pair individual inputs and outputs, but some machines are controlled through combinations of actuators. A steady mapping \(y=Gu\) with adequate static rank can still require a large actuator change in a particular direction. Scale inputs by allowed physical excursions and outputs by meaningful error or command scales. After scaling, the Euclidean norm is interpretable and singular values quantify directional gains. Without scaling, changing position units from metres to millimetres could dominate the result.

For a real square nonsingular \(G\), the symmetric positive definite matrix \(G^TG\) has orthonormal eigenvectors \(v_j\) and positive eigenvalues \(\sigma_j^2\). A unit actuator pattern \(v_j\) yields an output of length \(\sigma_j\) in direction \(u_j=Gv_j/\sigma_j\). Writing \(G=U\Sigma V^T\) then gives a transparent inverse. A desired output \(y_r\) requires actuator components \((u_j^Ty_r)/\sigma_j\) along \(v_j\). The smallest singular value therefore controls the largest gain of the inverse, and the ratio \(\sigma_{\max}/\sigma_{\min}\) measures conditioning.

This geometry describes steady input effort, not necessarily dynamic stability. Two channels with different lags can have different difficult directions at higher frequencies. The singular values of the complex frequency-response matrix can reveal that change after consistent scaling at every frequency.

Allocate two actuators with a tight current budget

Consider a scaled steady plant \(G=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0.9\\0.9&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). Its symmetry gives unit common and differential directions \((1,1)/\sqrt2\) and \((1,-1)/\sqrt2\), with singular values 1.9 and 0.1. A common output target \((0.2,0.2)\) requires only \(u=(0.1053,0.1053)\). A differential target \((0.2,-0.2)\) needs \(u=(2,-2)\), a factor nineteen more effort for the same output norm. If each drive is limited to magnitude one, the first target is feasible and the second is not.

One possible allocation trades exact tracking against effort by minimising \(\|Gu-y_r\|^2+\lambda\|u\|^2\). The stationary normal equations are \((G^TG+\lambda I)u=G^Ty_r\); each inverse singular value is replaced by \(\sigma_j/(\sigma_j^2+\lambda)\). For \(\lambda=0.01\) and the differential target, that factor is \(0.1/(0.01+0.01)=5\). The resulting input is \((1,-1)\) and output \((0.1,-0.1)\). The allocation respects these particular bounds while openly accepting half the requested differential change. A penalty does not enforce an input limit for every possible target. If exact limit enforcement is required, solve the bounded allocation problem or constrain the admissible command directions explicitly.

A simple perturbation test makes the weak direction visible in hardware. Command \(u=(0.1,0.1)\) in scaled units and predict \(y=(0.19,0.19)\). Reversing only the second command gives \(u=(0.1,-0.1)\) and outputs only \((0.01,-0.01)\). If each sensor has an uncertainty of ±0.01 output units, the first test has a comfortable signal level but the second barely resolves its predicted value. A poorly estimated differential gain may therefore vary considerably between identification records even though the common gain is repeatable. Increasing the differential test amplitude within operating bounds or averaging repeated experiments improves that estimate. A static inverse based on a single small pulse at each actuator may otherwise amplify calibration error precisely in the least observable output combination.

Chapter 12 · Finite-horizon control with real actuator constraints

Build a small predictive controller from a sampled model and determine when the requested target is physically reachable.

From first principles

Predict a finite trajectory before choosing the first command

Consider a scalar sampled plant \(x_{k+1}=ax_k+bu_k\), with a measured initial state and known coefficients. A predictive controller uses the model repeatedly to compare candidate future command sequences. For two steps, substitute the first state update into the second; each input affects a different interval and the first input also affects the second predicted state through propagation.

\[x_1=ax_0+bu_0,\]
\[x_2=a^2x_0+abu_0+bu_1 .\]

Even a one-step horizon shows how a quadratic objective selects a command. Let \(r\) be the desired next state, \(q>0\) a tracking penalty and \(\rho>0\) an effort penalty. Differentiate the objective after substituting its predicted state.

\[J=q(ax_0+bu_0-r)^2+\rho u_0^2,\]
\[u_0^*=\frac{qb(r-ax_0)}{qb^2+\rho}.\]

For this single scalar step, an interval limit on \(u_0\) clips the unconstrained minimiser to the nearest feasible endpoint. Over longer horizons the state and input constraints couple the commands. Only the first chosen command is applied before the state is measured again and the prediction repeated.

Derivation and applications

Predict the stopping distance of a sampled axis

A predictive controller can satisfy every instantaneous position bound and still enter a state from which the next position constraint must be violated. To see the role of velocity, consider a frictionless axis moving towards a positive position limit. The command is constant over sample interval \(h\) and bounded in acceleration by \(a>0\). Suppose the current speed \(v\) is positive, the axis must stop without reversing, and a final fractional braking command is allowed. Constant acceleration integrates exactly over each step.

\[v_{k+1}=v_k+h u_k,\]
\[x_{k+1}=x_k+h v_k+\tfrac12 h^2u_k,\]
\[|u_k|\leq a .\]

Apply maximum braking for \(n=\lfloor v/(ah)\rfloor\) full samples. The remaining speed \(w=v-nah\) is in \([0,ah)\). During these \(n\) samples the speed decreases linearly, so the travelled distance is their duration times the average of initial and final speed.

\[d_n=nh\,\frac{v+w}{2}=\frac{v^2-w^2}{2a}.\]

If \(w>0\), select \(u=-w/h\) for the last interval. The last step travels \(hw/2\) and ends at rest exactly on a sampling instant. Combining the two contributions yields the minimum stopping distance with this no-reversal and held-command requirement.

\[d_{\rm stop}=\frac{v^2}{2a}+\frac{w(ah-w)}{2a}.\]

Maximum early braking minimises future speeds and hence the travelled distance. The extra term compared with continuous switching is nonnegative and vanishes for an integer number of full braking intervals. A position-only terminal constraint can overlook this extra travel.

Worked example

Check if a carriage can stop before its limit

Let \(v=0.550\) m/s, \(a=2.00\) m/s² and \(h=0.100\) s. Two full braking steps leave \(w=0.150\) m/s. They consume \(0.200(0.550+0.150)/2=0.0700\) m. The final step needs \(u=-1.50\) m/s² and travels 7.50 mm, giving 77.5 mm total. A remaining clearance of 76.0 mm exceeds the continuous stopping distance of 75.6 mm but is insufficient for the sampled axis.

Second application

Bound the error without knowing the initial speed

For \(a=1.20\) m/s² and \(h=0.200\) s the correction is largest at \(w=ah/2\). Its maximum is \(ah^2/8=6.00\) mm. At \(v=0.360\) m/s a full step leaves exactly 0.120 m/s, so the continuous estimate of 54.0 mm increases to 60.0 mm. Reducing the sample interval by half reduces this worst-case excess distance by a factor of four.

Predict a sequence before applying the first action

For \(x_{k+1}=Ax_k+Bu_k\) with measured or estimated \(x_k\), successive substitutions give \(x_{k+2}=A^2x_k+ABu_k+Bu_{k+1}\). The initial state sets the free response; the planned input sequence contributes linearly. With quadratic tracking and effort penalties, linear dynamics and linear bounds on states or inputs, planning becomes a convex quadratic programme. At each update apply only the first planned command, acquire a new measurement and solve again. Receding the horizon provides feedback when disturbances make actual motion depart from the earlier prediction.

Solve a two-step constrained problem

Take the dimensionless integrator \(x_{k+1}=x_k+u_k\), \(x_k=2\), and target zero. Minimise \(x_{k+1}^2+x_{k+2}^2+u_k^2+u_{k+1}^2\). With \(u=u_k,v=u_{k+1}\), the objective is \((2+u)^2+(2+u+v)^2+u^2+v^2\). Its stationary equations are \(3u+v=-4,\ u+2v=-2\). The unconstrained plan \(u=-1.2,\ v=-0.4\) reaches states 0.8 and 0.4. If both input magnitudes are limited to one, the first command is infeasible. Fixing \(u=-1\) and reoptimising the second gives \(v=-0.5\), reaching 1 and 0.5. The derivative with respect to \(u\) points toward smaller \(u\) at this boundary, confirming the active limit.

After applying −1, suppose the new state is exactly 1. The next two-step unconstrained plan scales the original one by one half, giving first action −0.6. The earlier second action was −0.5. This change is consistent even without disturbances, since the new optimisation includes a further future step. A terminal cost approximating the remaining cost of control can reduce this artificial dependence on the planning horizon.

Feasible commands and feasible trajectories differ

A command bound always has feasible choices if zero lies within it. Adding a hard state constraint, such as a position stop, may make all predicted trajectories infeasible from the current state. Before asking for a time-optimal move, compute elementary reachability bounds. With \(|u|\le1\), a scalar integrator can move at most \(N\) units in \(N\) samples. A terminal constraint demanding zero from state 3 in two samples is impossible. Allowing slack in a comfort-related temperature band may be defensible; a physical actuator limit must still be enforced. Report which constraints were active and what performance was sacrificed.

Close the engineering loop

For a practical comparison, first establish a verified baseline with the OneSimulation proportional-loop record. Reconstruct its pole and finite-record bias from the model parameters. This record witnesses how an unconstrained linear loop behaves; it does not itself exercise a predictive optimiser. Now design a separate held-input or constrained test whose command reaches a known bound, record measured timestamps and actuator signals, and compare with a by-hand prediction of the first active limit. The useful performance claims differ: accurate steady tracking, stable sampled dynamics, adequate estimator uncertainty and feasibility under limits each require an observation capable of testing them.

Derive a terminal region that can be maintained indefinitely

A finite-horizon planner can reach a state at its last predicted sample that looks inexpensive but has no feasible continuation. A terminal set provides a constructive remedy. For \(x_{k+1}=Ax_k+Bu_k\), choose local feedback \(u=Kx\). A set \(X_f\) is positively invariant under this feedback if every \(x\in X_f\) satisfies the state and input bounds and \((A+BK)x\in X_f\). Requiring the last predicted state to belong to this set gives the next optimisation a candidate, provided the model is exact and a feasible plan already exists.

To see this, suppose the accepted plan contains controls \(u_0,\ldots,u_{N-1}\) and states \(x_0,\ldots,x_N\in X\), with \(x_N\in X_f\). Apply \(u_0\). At the next time, shift the remaining controls one step and append \(Kx_N\). Its last state is \((A+BK)x_N\), still in \(X_f\). Every intermediate state and command came from the old feasible plan. This argument establishes recursive feasibility for the ideal deterministic system; the cost can then be chosen to establish a separate performance or stability claim.

For an unstable scalar plant \(x^+=1.2x+u\), bounds \(|u|\le0.5,\ |x|\le2\), choose \(u=-0.7x\). The resulting factor is 0.5. Choosing \(|x|\le0.5/0.7=5/7\) protects the local input limit and the next-state magnitude contracts. This interval is one admissible terminal set; it need not be the largest invariant region obtainable with all possible controls.

Construct the two-step feasible region by working backwards

For the same plant, suppose a terminal interval is \([-b,b]\). A current state can reach it in one step if the reachable interval \([1.2x-0.5,1.2x+0.5]\) intersects \([-b,b]\). Solving the two endpoint inequalities gives \(|x|\le(b+0.5)/1.2\), additionally restricted by \(|x|\le2\). Starting from \(b_0=5/7=0.7143\), the one-step interval has half-width \(b_1=1.0119\), and the two-step interval has \(b_2=1.2599\). A current error \(x=1.20\) is therefore feasible for two steps but cannot reach the terminal set in one.

A feasible two-step plan applies \(u_0=u_1=-0.5\). It generates \(x_1=0.94,\ x_2=0.628\), inside the terminal interval. The appended local command is \(-0.7(0.628)=-0.440\), generating \(x_3=0.314\). From \(x=1.40\), however, even two maximal negative commands yield \(x_1=1.18,\ x_2=0.916>0.7143\). A solver reporting infeasibility there has identified a physical horizon limitation. Lengthening the horizon can enlarge the feasible region. External disturbances and estimator error can spoil the exact shifted-plan construction, motivating reserve inside the hard bounds or an explicitly disturbance-aware terminal set.

Observe the limiting sequence of backwards-reachable interval widths. Ignoring the physical state cap for a moment, the recursion has fixed point \(b=(b+0.5)/1.2=2.5\). At \(x=2.5\) the strongest negative input merely holds position, and beyond it even that input allows outward divergence. With the stated state limit the physically relevant bound is tighter at 2.0. From \(x=2.0\) the strongest input moves to 1.9, so that boundary can be brought inward over enough samples. This examination distinguishes insufficient horizon from insufficient actuator authority. A solver change cannot rescue a state above the unrestricted 2.5 threshold with the given input, whereas increasing horizon can rescue a feasible physical state that lies just beyond the current backwards-reachable interval.

Now suppose implementation records establish an additive one-step disturbance bound \(|w|\le0.05\) in \(x^+=1.2x+u+w\). At local feedback \(u=-0.7x\), guaranteeing that \([-b,b]\) maps to itself under every such disturbance requires \(0.5b+0.05\le b\). The terminal interval above satisfies this because \(b=0.7143>0.10\), and the feedback still respects the input limit. To guarantee that a chosen predecessor reaches this interval in one step, the nominal next state needs to lie within \([-b+0.05,b-0.05]\). The robust predecessor half-width is thus \((b-0.05+0.5)/1.2=0.970\), smaller than the nominal 1.012.

Applying the backward rule twice gives the robust two-step half-width \((0.970-0.05+0.5)/1.2=1.183\) when an updated action can be selected at the intermediate state. The previously feasible initial state 1.20 is outside that guarantee. Indeed maximal negative actuation with two consecutive positive disturbances yields \(x_1=0.99,\ x_2=0.738>0.7143\). A nominally feasible trajectory and an invariant terminal set alone therefore do not make the whole finite-horizon plan robust. The intermediate constraints and the information available when choosing the next input must also be included in the guarantee.

Locate the first active constraint in a predictive policy

For \(x^+=x+u\), penalise the sum of squared future states and inputs. In one step the unconstrained action is \(-x/2\). Eliminating the second action in a two-step plan gives first action \(-3x/5\). Solve again on the active face once the input bound is reached.

Problem sheet 6 · Coupled plants and constrained predictions

End the course by testing an actuator pairing, a finite-horizon plan and a specification that initially looks feasible.

1. Unequal heat paths

For \(G=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}3&1\\2&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), (a) compute its relative gain array. (b) Calculate both actuator changes needed to raise output 1 by one unit while holding output 2 fixed. (c) Are these changes available if both actuators already sit at their lower bounds?

Solve the outputs before choosing a controller

The determinant is 4, so the array has diagonal entries \(6/4=1.5\) and cross entries \(-2/4=-0.5\). The desired change \(G^{-1}(1,0)^\mathsf{T}=(0.5,-0.5)^\mathsf{T}\) needs the second heater to reduce its command. At its lower bound this is infeasible. Reassigning loop pairings does not remove this physical constraint.

2. Active limits in a predictive move

Use the chapter's two-step integrator problem with initial state 3 and \(|u|,|v|\le1\). (a) Find the unconstrained plan. (b) Optimise the second action when the first is at its lower bound and compute the objective. (c) Can a requirement \(x_{k+2}=0\) be added?

Check the unconstrained optimum, then the active face

Scaling the unconstrained plan gives \((-1.8,-0.6)\). Fix \(u=-1\); the remaining derivative is \(2(2+v)+2v\), giving \(v=-1\), which is feasible. The input gradients at the lower bounds are nonnegative (the second is zero), establishing optimality for the convex objective. The states are 2 and 1, so the cost is \(4+1+1+1=7\). The largest two-step reduction is 2, so the terminal equality is infeasible.

3. A requirement beyond actuator authority

A speed loop has steady plant \(y=2u-d\), with \(|u|\le2\), opposing disturbance \(d=1\) and desired speed 4 in normalised units. (a) Test whether any controller can track that command indefinitely. (b) Find the best possible steady output and error. (c) Give the measurements needed to distinguish saturation from an incorrect model gain in a logged experiment.

Apply the balance before retuning the loop

Steady tracking requires \(u=(4+1)/2=2.5\), beyond the upper bound. The maximum steady speed is 3, leaving error 1. Log the controller's requested action, the delivered actuator signal, the load and the measured response. Verify the gain using smaller feasible steady commands; if delivered and requested signals diverge at the limit, the balance predicts the observed loss of tracking. Increasing integral gain cannot change the feasible steady range.

Commission a sampled loop with a limited actuator

A first-order plant has unit gain and time constant 1 s. A proportional controller of gain 3 holds its command for h seconds. With a unit reference and zero initial output, find the steady error, the sampling limit and the first three sampled outputs for h = 0.50 s. Then limit the command to [0, 1]. Predict the first sample at which the upper limit releases.

Worked solution

Integration during one hold gives yₖ₊₁ = ayₖ+(1−a)uₖ with a = exp(−h). Before clipping, uₖ = 3(1−yₖ), the pole is 4a−3, and the equilibrium is 0.75. The error 0.25 is independent of h for a stable loop. Requiring the pole to exceed −1 gives h less than ln 2 seconds. At h = 0.50 the pole is −0.5739; the other stability inequality holds for positive h.

The first three outputs are 0.75[1−(4a−3)ᵏ] for k = 1, 2 and 3, approximately 1.180, 0.503 and 0.892. The first unconstrained command is 3. After clipping the actuator stays at one until a sample reaches y ≥ 2/3, when the requested input no longer exceeds one.

While the limit is active the output ticks follow yₖ = 1−exp(−0.5k). At k = 2 the output is 0.632 and the controller still requests 1.104. At k = 3 the output is 0.777 and the request is 0.669. The upper limit releases at 1.5 s. In the course experiment compare the unlimited trajectory first. Applying the scalar pole relation across a saturated interval would identify a different dynamics.

Chapter 13 · Proving a region of attraction with the energy of a pendulum

Pole locations describe a small neighbourhood of a nonlinear equilibrium. An energy argument can prove that a much larger set of initial conditions converges to the same resting point. Work out the region explicitly and test individual trajectories against its boundary.

Extract a storage function from a torque balance

Measure angle \(\theta\) from the downward vertical, let \(\omega=\dot\theta\), and use pivot inertia J and gravitational torque coefficient \(K=mgl\). Add viscous damping b and a control torque \(u=-k_d\omega\) that opposes motion. The closed dynamics are \(J\dot\omega=-K\sin\theta-(b+k_d)\omega\). Define a scalar function with units of energy by adding kinetic energy to the integral of the restoring torque from zero angle:

\[V(\theta,\omega)={1\over2}J\omega^2+K(1-\cos\theta),\]
\[\dot V=J\omega\dot\omega+K\sin\theta\,\omega=-(b+k_d)\omega^2 .\]

A positive damping coefficient ensures energy cannot increase. On the branch \(-\pi<\theta<\pi\), V is positive away from the hanging rest state and zero there. Its derivative is zero whenever velocity vanishes, including ordinary turning points. To justify eventual convergence we need to establish whether any motion can remain indefinitely in this zero-derivative set.

Distinguish a turning point from an invariant state

At \(\omega=0\) a nonzero \(\sin\theta\) produces angular acceleration, taking the trajectory immediately away from zero velocity. Inside the well the only state remaining forever at zero velocity is \((0,0)\). The upright position sits at potential energy \(2K\). Each closed sublevel \(V\le c<2K\) containing the downward rest state is compact and cannot leave the well, because reaching \(\theta=\pm\pi\) would cost at least \(2K\). LaSalle's invariance principle then establishes asymptotic convergence on that component.

\[|\omega_0|<\sqrt{{2K\over J}(1+\cos\theta_0)}\]

This expresses the strict sublevel as a speed allowance at each angle. A state outside it can still settle in the same well because damping removes energy before it reaches the top. The inequality is a sufficient certificate that can be tested from an initial measurement. Exactly at the upright rest state no damping torque acts and the system can remain there.

Compare two releases at the same angle

Let \(J=0.20\) kg m², \(K=2.0\) N m and \(b+k_d=0.30\) N m s/rad. At 60° the potential energy is 1.0 J and the certificate allows speeds below \(\sqrt{30}=5.48\) rad/s. At 4 rad/s the total is 2.6 J, safely below the 4 J barrier. The turning angle cannot exceed \(\arccos(1-2.6/2)=107.5^\circ\) in magnitude. Positive damping reduces the actual maximum further.

At 6 rad/s the initial energy is 4.6 J, outside that guarantee. The instantaneous dissipation is \(0.30(6)^2=10.8\) W, so dissipation before any top crossing may be substantial. Integrate the equation with the same initial angle and compare the true energy trace with the barrier. Moving the damping slider changes the rate of energy loss, while the sublevel calculated from J and K remains fixed.

Raise the starting speed until the initial energy just exceeds the barrier. Compare a damped run with a zero-damping run. Use the two traces to distinguish a guaranteed sublevel from energy actually removed during a particular trajectory.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial · design an admissible release speed

At 120° with the same parameters, choose a speed ensuring the trajectory remains below 90% of the barrier. What changes in the proof if the velocity gain cancels the physical damping exactly?

Show the energy budget

The potential energy is \(2(1-\cos120^\circ)=3.0\) J. The target maximum total energy is 3.6 J, leaving 0.6 J of kinetic energy. Thus \(|\omega_0|\le\sqrt{2(0.6)/0.20}=2.45\) rad/s to the quoted precision. When \(b+k_d=0\), energy is constant. The sublevel remains invariant, but whole undamped orbits now lie in the zero-derivative set, so convergence to rest no longer follows.

Further reading · Åström and Murray, Feedback Systems · chapter 5, Lyapunov stability

Chapter 14 · Why integral action rejects an unknown constant load

A motor regulates speed while an unknown torque is applied to its shaft. A proportional controller allows a steady speed offset. Add a state that remembers the offset and derive the complete load transient as well as its eventual rejection.

Find the equilibrium before tuning poles

Use \(J\dot y+by=u-d\), where y is angular speed, u is motor torque, and d is an opposing load. For \(u=K_p(r-y)\) at constant reference r and load d the steady speed is \((K_pr-d)/(b+K_p)\). With r=0 and a positive load the speed settles below zero in these deviation variables. A constant motor torque is needed to cancel the load, yet pure proportional control at zero error produces zero torque.

Introduce \(z' = r-y\) and \(u=K_p(r-y)+K_i z\). At any equilibrium the condition \(z'=0\) requires \(y=r\); the plant equation then requires \(K_i z=br+d\). The controller state can store the torque needed at zero error. This reasoning assumes available actuator torque and closed-loop stability. If the actuator cannot supply \(br+d\), no such regulated equilibrium exists.

Place the poles and solve a disturbance step

Set r=0 and transform from initial equilibrium with zero deviations. Eliminating z gives

\[{Y(s)\over D(s)}={-s\over Js^2+(b+K_p)s+K_i},\]
\[K_i=J\omega_n^2,\]
\[K_p=2J\zeta\omega_n-b .\]

The numerator zero at s=0 removes constant disturbances in steady state. It follows from the integrator pole in the open loop. For a load step \(D=d_0/s\), the initial rate is \(-d_0/J\) regardless of gains because the feedback states have not had time to change. For a repeated real pole at \(-a\), inversion yields

\[y(t)=-{d_0\over J}\,t e^{-at},\]
\[t_{\rm peak}=1/a,\]
\[|y_{\rm peak}|={d_0\over Jea}.\]

A peak and recovery are expected even with exact final rejection. Increasing a improves that peak in the ideal model, at the price of higher control gains and possible sensitivity to sensor noise or actuator limits.

Quantify how a speed loop recovers

Let \(J=0.20\) kg m², \(b=0.50\) N m s/rad and choose \(a=4\) s⁻¹. The critical gains are \(K_p=1.10\) N m s/rad and \(K_i=3.20\) N m/rad. A step of 2 N m gives a peak deviation of −0.920 rad/s after 0.25 s. At 1 s it has recovered to −0.183 rad/s and ultimately reaches zero. The stored state supplies exactly 2 N m once speed has recovered.

With the same \(K_p\) but no integral action, the step gives \(y(t)=-2[1-e^{-(1.6/0.2)t}]/1.6\), tending to −1.25 rad/s. Its initial slope is also −10 rad/s². Use the two traces to identify an interval where a short measurement could make the controllers look similar, and compare their final speed offsets. For a disturbance of the opposite sign the curves reverse by linearity.

Double the pole rate and predict the new peak time and peak magnitude. Compare the very first slopes of PI and proportional-only responses. Which of the measurements could reveal increased bandwidth and which depends only on inertia?

Tutorial · pick poles from a peak-error specification

A 1.5 N m load step must produce at most 0.50 rad/s peak error with \(J=0.20\) kg m² and the same b. Choose the smallest critical pole rate meeting this condition and calculate the gains. What torque must remain available after the transient?

Show the bound and gains

Using the exact peak gives \(a\ge1.5/[0.20e(0.50)]=5.52\) s⁻¹. Thus \(K_i\simeq6.09\) and \(K_p\simeq1.71\) in the units above. The motor needs at least 1.5 N m of extra steady torque to reject the new load at the original speed. Pole placement alone cannot establish the available transient or steady torque margin.

Chapter 15 · Separating reference tracking from disturbance rejection

A positioning or speed loop can reject a load well but follow a demanded trajectory poorly. Add a feedforward signal computed from the requested motion, and derive precisely how mismatch and command smoothness affect tracking.

Use a physical balance to predict the required input

Take a first-order plant \(J\dot y+by=u-d\) with positive inertia and drag. Suppose the commanded speed \(r(t)\) and its derivative are available from a trajectory generator. From the plant balance the model input that would exactly sustain \(y=r\) at zero disturbance is \(\widehat J\dot r+\widehat b r\), where hats indicate estimated parameters. Add ordinary proportional correction on the tracking error \(e=r-y\):

\[u=\widehat J\dot r+\widehat b r+K_pe,\]
\[J\dot e+(b+K_p)e=(J-\widehat J)\dot r+(b-\widehat b)r+d .\]

This error equation separates three causes of deviation. Feedback changes the decay rate and attenuates each forcing term. With exact parameters, zero disturbance and correct initial conditions, arbitrary differentiable trajectories are tracked exactly in this ideal model. A discontinuous step calls for an impulse through the derivative term; a finite actuator cannot follow it. A generated smooth trajectory supplies a realizable alternative.

Design a command with finite endpoint slopes

Move from zero to target R during an interval T with \(r=R(3s^2-2s^3)\), \(s=t/T\). It has zero velocity derivative at both ends and peak \(\dot r=1.5 R/T\) at the midpoint. Doubling T halves the inertial part of the nominal torque without changing the final drag torque. An acceleration or torque budget can therefore determine trajectory duration before feedback gains are selected. A sensor derivative of a noisy measured speed is unnecessary here because \(\dot r\) is computed from the commanded polynomial.

For a constant-slope segment \(r=vt\), suppose drag is estimated exactly and the load is zero. The mismatch forcing is \((J-\widehat J)v\). Away from segment transitions the settled tracking lag is \((J-\widehat J)v/(b+K_p)\). At a hold after the segment the mismatch forcing disappears, and this lag decays with time constant \(J/(b+K_p)\).

A tracking error that reveals the wrong inertia

Let \(J=0.20\), \(b=0.50\) and \(K_p=1.5\) in consistent SI units. The tracking time constant is 0.10 s. With \(\widehat J=0.15\), correct drag and a 4 rad/s² ramp, the lag approaches \(0.05(4)/2=0.10\) rad/s. After 0.30 s it reaches \(0.10(1-e^{-3})=0.0950\) rad/s. A constant opposing load of 0.40 N m adds a steady 0.20 rad/s lag regardless of this inertia mismatch. Correcting feedforward inertia can improve tracking without changing load rejection.

For a cubic transition to 4 rad/s in 1 s, the midpoint has \(r=2\) rad/s and \(\dot r=6\) rad/s². The nominal motor torque at that instant is \(0.20(6)+0.50(2)=2.20\) N m. Repeating the move in 2 s reduces the midpoint request to 1.60 N m; the final torque remains 2.0 N m for both durations. The plot integrates the error equation, so it also captures lag while the slope changes.

Set estimated inertia equal to the actual 0.20 kg m² before changing move duration. Then halve the estimate and compare lag during acceleration and during the hold. Explain how shortening move duration tests the available torque separately from feedback damping.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial · distinguish drag bias from inertia bias

With no external load, a long constant-speed hold at 4 rad/s leaves a 0.10 rad/s lag. Use the same true b and tuned \(K_p\) to estimate \(\widehat b\). Can a measurement during this hold identify the feedforward inertia as well?

Show the identification argument

At constant reference \(\dot r=0\) and at equilibrium \(\dot e=0\). Thus \(0.10=(0.50-\widehat b)(4)/2\), giving \(\widehat b=0.45\) N m s/rad. The inertia term vanishes identically on this data segment. A segment with nonzero \(\dot r\), ideally repeated at opposite slopes at the same speed, supplies an independent observation of inertial mismatch.

Further reading · Åström and Murray, Feedback Systems · chapter 12, feedforward design

Chapter 16 · How much transport delay a feedback loop can tolerate

A communication or transport delay changes phase at every frequency while leaving gain untouched. Use that property to derive the first stability boundary for a specific loop and to interpret the phase margin in units of seconds.

Locate the critical imaginary-axis crossing

Use negative unity feedback with \(L(s)=K e^{-s\tau}/(1+sT)\), where K is dimensionless, T is a plant time constant and \(\tau\) is a pure delay. With \(\tau=0\) and positive K the single closed-loop pole is \(-(1+K)/T\). At a purely imaginary characteristic root \(s=j\omega\), the condition \(1+L(j\omega)=0\) requires both unit magnitude and an odd multiple of \(-\pi\) phase. Since the delay has magnitude one, the unique positive gain-crossover frequency for \(K>1\) obeys

\[{K\over\sqrt{1+(\omega_cT)^2}}=1,\]
\[\omega_c={\sqrt{K^2-1}\over T}.\]

The undelayed plant contributes \(-\arctan(\omega_cT)\). The delay contributes an unwrapped phase \(-\omega_c\tau\) in radians. The first critical delay therefore satisfies

\[\tau_*={\pi-\arctan(\omega_c T)\over\omega_c},\]
\[\mathrm{PM}(\tau)=\pi-\arctan(\omega_cT)-\omega_c\tau .\]

Further crossings at the same frequency would require another \(2\pi\) of delay phase. The first one governs the delay margin because the zero-delay system is stable. For \(0<K<1\) no positive frequency reaches unit gain in this particular first-order model. At \(K=1\) the only unit magnitude occurs at zero frequency where the phase is zero.

Check that the first crossing enters instability

The characteristic equation can be written \(1+sT+K e^{-s\tau}=0\). Implicit differentiation and substitution of \(K e^{-s\tau}=-(1+sT)\) give \(ds/d\tau=-s(1+sT)/[T+\tau(1+sT)]\). To test the direction it is simpler to invert this derivative:

\[\left({ds\over d\tau}\right)^{-1}=-{T\over s(1+sT)}-{\tau\over s},\]
\[\Re\left[\left({ds\over d\tau}\right)^{-1}\right]_{s=j\omega_c}={T^2\over1+\omega_c^2T^2}>0.\]

A complex number and its reciprocal have real parts of the same sign. Thus the conjugate pair enters the right half-plane when the delay increases past the first critical value. The oscillation period there is \(2\pi/\omega_c\).

Latency budget at two loop gains

With \(T=0.50\) s and \(K=2\), \(\omega_c=3.464\) rad/s. The undelayed phase at crossover is −60°, leaving 120° or 2.094 rad. The critical delay is 0.605 s and the crossing period is 1.81 s. An installation with 0.30 s of delay retains about 60.5° of phase margin.

Raise K to 4 with the same T. Then \(\omega_c=7.746\) rad/s, the undelayed phase is −75.5°, and the delay margin falls to 0.235 s. The same 0.30 s installation would cross the stability boundary. Reading a phase plot wrapped modulo 360° can conceal such a crossing. Use the unwrapped curves and locate the frequency where gain equals one before judging stability.

At fixed loop gain increase delay until the reported margin vanishes. Use the frequency of that point to estimate the oscillation period. Repeat after increasing gain and check whether the same physical time delay spends the same phase budget.

Measure how a gain change affects the undelayed first-order closed loop in the course laboratory. Then use its increased bandwidth to explain the reduced delay budget calculated here.

Tutorial · allocate a reserve in degrees

With \(K=2,\ T=0.50\) s require at least 45° phase margin. Calculate the largest total delay and the spare time when fixed transport already takes 0.20 s. Explain the effect of using degrees directly in \(\omega\tau\).

Show the phase budget

Subtract the required 45° from the undelayed 120°, leaving 75° for delay. Converting to radians gives \(1.309/3.464=0.378\) s total. The remaining budget is about 0.178 s. The expression \(\omega\tau\) is in radians for \(\omega\) in rad/s. Dividing the number 75 directly by 3.464 would overestimate the budget by \(180/\pi\).

Further reading · Åström and Murray, Feedback Systems · chapter 10, Nyquist criterion and stability margins

Chapter 17 · Why a stable plant can initially move in the wrong direction

Two processes may act on a measured output at different speeds and with opposite signs. Their combined output can first fall after a positive command and later rise. Derive that transient and see why trying to cancel its zero creates an unstable internal state.

Build a right-half-plane zero from two stable responses

Let a unit input drive two stable first-order processes in parallel. A slow path has gain \(a>0\) and time constant \(T_s\); a fast path is subtracted, with gain \(b>0\) and time constant \(T_f<T_s\). The measured output transfer function is

\[G(s)={a\over1+sT_s}-{b\over1+sT_f}={(a-b)+(aT_f-bT_s)s\over(1+sT_s)(1+sT_f)}.\]

Choose \(a>b\) so the final step response is positive. Its initial slope is \(a/T_s-b/T_f\). When this slope is negative, \(bT_s>aT_f\), so the numerator has a real positive zero \(z=(a-b)/(bT_s-aT_f)\). Each storage process has a stable pole. The right-half-plane zero expresses the way their contributions are combined in the output.

Locate the undershoot without a numerical search

For a unit step apply each path's time-domain solution and subtract. Setting the time derivative to zero and taking logarithms gives the time of minimum.

\[y(t)=a(1-e^{-t/T_s})-b(1-e^{-t/T_f}),\]
\[t_{\min}={\ln[bT_s/(aT_f)]\over 1/T_f-1/T_s}.\]

For the inverse-response conditions this is positive. The steady value diagnosis alone would see only \(a-b\); a short test would reveal mostly the fast negative path. Identifying both requires measurements on both time scales. When the fast and slow slopes exactly match, the numerator loses its s term and the finite zero moves to infinity.

Read a time-domain constraint directly from the zero

For a bounded causal unit-step response \(Y(s)=G(s)/s\). At the zero \(Y(z)=0\), hence \(\int_0^\infty e^{-zt}y(t)\,dt=0\). An output ultimately settling to a positive constant cannot stay positive at every time while obeying this weighted integral. A controller implementing the exact inverse \(1/G\) would have a pole at z and an exponentially growing response to small errors in its internal state. Shaping the command over a longer interval can reduce an undershoot without introducing that unstable inversion.

Two experiments with the same final reading

Take \(a=2,\ b=1,\ T_s=4\) s and \(T_f=0.50\) s. The zero is at \(1/3\) s⁻¹. The initial slope is \(0.5-2=-1.5\) s⁻¹ and the minimum occurs at 0.792 s. Evaluating the response gives about −0.435 before it ultimately reaches +1. This initial dip can look like a sensor wired with the wrong sign until the slow path has had time to act.

Lengthen the fast path to 1.5 s. The same final value +1 now accompanies a less negative initial slope of −0.167 s⁻¹. The zero moves to \(1/(4-3)=1\) s⁻¹ and the minimum occurs at 0.690 s with an undershoot about −0.052. Try moving the fast time constant through 2 s. Predict the initial slope and disappearance of the undershoot before examining the curves.

Increase the fast time constant through the equal-slope value of 2 s at the default gains. Predict the disappearance of the negative excursion. Reduce the subtracted gain and derive the new equal-slope condition before repeating the test.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial · infer a hidden fast time constant

A fitted slow path has \(a=2,\ T_s=4\) s. The steady step response is 1 and the measured initial slope is −0.75 s⁻¹. Infer b, \(T_f\), the zero position and the time of minimum.

Show the parameter recovery

The final reading gives \(b=a-1=1\). The initial slope gives \(1/T_f=2/4+0.75=1.25\) s⁻¹, so \(T_f=0.80\) s. The zero is \(1/(4-1.6)=0.417\) s⁻¹ and \(t_{\min}=\ln(4/1.6)/(1.25-0.25)=0.916\) s. Combining a static observation with a dynamic one makes both path parameters identifiable in this ideal two-path model.

Further reading · Åström and Murray, Feedback Systems · chapter 14 on fundamental limits

Chapter 18 · Designing a lead network at a specified gain crossover

A loop is too lightly damped at the bandwidth needed for tracking. A lead network can supply positive phase over a frequency band. Locate the band mathematically, set its gain crossover deliberately and measure the accompanying high-frequency gain increase.

Build a lead element from one pole and one zero

Take \(C(s)=K(1+sT)/(1+sT/\alpha)\) with \(\alpha>1\). The zero occurs at \(1/T\) and the pole at the higher frequency \(\alpha/T\). Its phase contribution is \(\phi=\arctan(\omega T)-\arctan(\omega T/\alpha)\). At very low or very high frequency that phase tends to zero. Differentiate with respect to \(z=\omega T\) to locate the interior maximum:

\[{1\over1+z^2}-{1/\alpha\over1+z^2/\alpha^2}=0,\]
\[z=\sqrt{\alpha},\]
\[\sin\phi_{\max}={\alpha-1\over\alpha+1}.\]

The maximum occurs at the geometric mean of the zero and pole frequencies. At this same frequency the magnitude of the dynamic part is \(\sqrt\alpha\), obtained by substituting \(z^2=\alpha\) into \(\sqrt{(1+z^2)/(1+z^2/\alpha^2)}\). Thus positive phase comes with a magnitude change. Adding lead without resetting scalar K can move crossover to a frequency where the anticipated plant phase and lead are different.

Set the phase and magnitude at a common design point

For a stable minimum-phase design example with an open-loop integrator, take \(G(s)=1/[s(1+s)]\) and select \(\omega_c=2\) rad/s. Plant phase there is \(-90^\circ-\arctan2=-153.43^\circ\). Choose 30° extra lead, giving nominal phase margin 56.57°. Invert the peak-phase relation and put the peak at the target frequency:

\[\alpha={1+\sin\phi_{\max}\over1-\sin\phi_{\max}},\]
\[T={\sqrt\alpha\over\omega_c},\]
\[K={1\over|G(i\omega_c)|\sqrt{\alpha}}.\]

A scalar-gain controller chosen for the same crossover would have \(K_P=1/|G(i\omega_c)|\) and margin only 26.57°. This comparison holds crossover fixed, so the phase advantage can be attributed to the compensator. The return ratio magnitude must still be checked away from crossover, especially for a plant with additional modes or delay.

Calculate the network and identify a robustness cost

For the 30° design \(\alpha=3,\ T=0.866\) s, giving zero and pole frequencies of 1.155 and 3.464 rad/s. Plant magnitude at 2 rad/s is \(1/(2\sqrt5)=0.2236\), so K=2.582. The high-frequency control gain tends to \(K\alpha=7.746\), while its low-frequency gain is 2.582. High-frequency measurement noise passed through a feedback controller can therefore induce larger actuator activity than a low-frequency calculation suggests. The actual noise transfer to the input must include the loop sensitivity.

Require a more aggressive 45° boost at the same crossover. Then \(\alpha=5.828,\ T=1.207\) s and \(K=1.852\). Margin becomes 71.57° and the high-frequency gain reaches 10.80. The larger boost also lowers the DC controller gain, which changes low-frequency disturbance attenuation in this plant. Comparing the two phase traces alongside their designed crossover avoids selecting a controller purely from its maximum phase number.

Keep crossover at 2 rad/s and increase the required phase from 30 to 45 degrees. Compare the high-frequency controller gains. Now fix lead and move crossover upward to inspect how plant phase can consume the added margin.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial · allow for a known delay

Return to the 30° design and insert a 0.10 s pure delay. Compute the remaining phase margin at 2 rad/s. What maximum additional delay is compatible with a required 40° margin? Why is there no gain crossover shift for this change?

Spend the phase reserve in radians

At crossover 0.10 s adds \(2(0.10)=0.20\) rad or 11.46° of phase lag. Margin falls to 45.11°. To retain 40° permits a further \((45.11-40)\pi/[180(2)]=0.0446\) s. Pure delay has unit magnitude at every frequency, so the designed crossover stays fixed. In contrast, adding a physical low-pass filter would change both gain and phase and require a fresh crossover calculation.

Further reading · Åström and Murray, Feedback Systems · chapter 12, feedback design by loop shaping

Chapter 19 · The period and duty cycle of a thermostat with hysteresis

A two-position thermostat can have a stable, repeatable temperature oscillation even though neither operating state has an equilibrium inside the chosen control band. Use event times to calculate the cycle and test its heat balance.

Couple a thermal balance to a discrete memory state

Let a uniform thermal mass have heat capacity C, conductance G to ambient at \(T_a\), and heater power P when the switch state \(s=1\). With heater off \(s=0\). The continuous state obeys \(C\dot T=-G(T-T_a)+Ps\). The relay turns on when cooling reaches \(T_-\), and turns off when heating reaches \(T_+>T_-\). Between thresholds it retains the most recent switch state. Thus specifying a temperature inside the band without the binary state does not fully specify the future.

\[\tau=C/G,\]
\[T_{\rm on}=T_a+P/G,\]
\[T_{\rm off}=T_a,\]
\[T(t)=T_{\rm eq}+[T(0)-T_{\rm eq}]e^{-t/\tau}.\]

Both thresholds must be reachable in their respective modes for periodic cycling: \(T_a<T_-<T_+<T_{\rm on}\). If \(T_{\rm on}=T_+\) the threshold is approached only asymptotically in this ideal model. A temperature outside the band initially can lead to a transient before the repeating orbit begins.

Solve the crossing conditions exactly

Start at \(T_-\) with the heater on. Solve the exponential trajectory for the time to \(T_+\). Switch state, set that endpoint as the cooling initial condition and solve for the return time. Each logarithm compares distances from the relevant equilibrium:

\[t_{\rm on}=\tau\ln{T_{\rm on}-T_-\over T_{\rm on}-T_+},\]
\[t_{\rm off}=\tau\ln{T_+-T_a\over T_--T_a},\]
\[D={t_{\rm on}\over t_{\rm on}+t_{\rm off}}.\]

Integrate the energy equation over one complete period. Net stored energy change is zero, so \(PD=G(\bar T-T_a)\). This gives the actual time-average temperature directly from the duty ratio. The arithmetic midpoint of the thresholds need not be the time average, because heating and cooling speeds vary across the swing.

Choose band width and estimate switching frequency

Let \(C=2000\) J/K, \(G=10\) W/K, \(P=200\) W and \(T_a=15^\circ\)C. The time constant is 200 s and the heating equilibrium is 35 °C. For thresholds 20 and 22 °C the on-time is \(200\ln(15/13)=28.62\) s and the off-time \(200\ln(7/5)=67.29\) s. Period is 95.9 s and duty 29.84%. Mean power is 59.7 W and the implied mean temperature 20.97 °C. The shorter heating interval reflects the larger temperature distance from the heated equilibrium.

Narrow the band to 20.5 and 21.5 °C at the same midpoint. The on-time falls to 14.29 s and off-time to 33.41 s. Switching frequency roughly doubles while duty remains close to 30%. In a real relay, sensor lag can permit overshoot beyond thresholds. A narrow hysteresis band then ceases to predict the observed extrema. Increasing numerical resolution in a continuous integrator only helps if its logic also detects the threshold crossing at the proper time.

Narrow the switching band by half while keeping its midpoint fixed. Predict the switching frequency and mean heating power, then read both event times. Raise heater power and identify which branch of the thermal trajectory should remain unchanged.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial · infer the thermal capacitance from one cycle

Suppose the wider band gives a measured cooling time of 80.0 s with ambient at 15 °C and the same conductance. Infer C and predict heating time if heater power remains 200 W. If the observed heating time disagrees but the cooling curve fits, identify one additional independent measurement.

Infer the time constant then check the energized mode

The cooling expression gives \(\tau=80/\ln(7/5)=237.8\) s and \(C=G\tau=2378\) J/K. Predicted heating time is \(237.8\ln(15/13)=34.0\) s. Measuring electrical heating power or the long-time temperature reached under a continuous on command tests \(P/G\). The cooling branch alone identifies the ratio C/G and does not determine that energized equilibrium.
